TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

As we’ve highlighted in multiple past editions of this newsletter, Hegseth has overseen one of the most dramatic reshapings of the U.S. military in its history—from goals and remit to personnel and culture, right down to what the department calls itself. On Wednesday, the defense secretary used his annual “State of the Force” speech in Quantico, Virginia, to tout what he’s done so far and lay out what he’s planning next. Hegseth unveiled several new initiatives aimed at “reviving the warrior ethos, rebuilding our military, and reestablishing deterrence.” Autonomy and technology. Hegseth announced a new combatant command focused on the military’s adoption of autonomous weapons. Known as Autonomous Warfare Command, or Autowarcom, the new command’s goal will be to “scale autonomous and robotic capabilities” across the military in what Hegseth said will be “the fastest peacetime shift in modern military history.” Hegseth also introduced an initiative called Project Meridian that aims to study the future of warfare. That project will be overseen by Emil Michael, the Defense Department’s chief technology officer, and co-led by Elon Musk, defense tech company Anduril’s founder Palmer Luckey, and former U.S. Speaker of the House Newt Gingrich, whom Hegseth described as “three of our nation’s best minds.” The project has 120 days to submit its findings and recommendations to Hegseth’s office. With tensions high, Paris is hoping that new austerity measures will rein in the country’s deficit and prevent opposition lawmakers from holding a no-confidence vote against another one of President Emmanuel Macron’s prime ministers. Political instability has long plagued Macron’s tenure. Since taking office in 2017, Macron has had seven premiers, the last two of whom were toppled over their unpopular austerity plans. From protests over farming protections to police brutality to pension reform , Paris has struggled to quell domestic unrest ahead of the country’s presidential election in 2027. Lecornu is hoping to change the tide. His proposal seeks to increase France’s defense budget from $64 billion to $71.2 billion while freezing public-sector wages and hiking taxes. “This budget enables us to get back on track towards consolidation through ​a significant effort,” Finance Minister Roland Lescure said, adding that savings were estimated to be worth around $60.7 billion in total. Whether that is enough to inject life back into the country’s sluggish economy, though, is unclear. France’s inflation rate rose to 3.4 percent year-on-year in September, its deficit is expected to hit 5.4 percent of GDP by the end of 2026, and 10-year borrowing costs reached nearly 5 percent on Thursday in their highest level since July 2002.   India’s opposition parties and the Cockroach movement are ready to march over fresh allegations of bias.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In 1951-52, independent India held its first free and fair election. As the country’s first chief election commissioner, Sukumar Sen—a civil servant who trained as a mathematician—was responsible for overseeing the voter rolls and ensuring the integrity of the vote. The task he faced was daunting: The Indian electorate at the time was composed of 176 million citizens , 85 percent of whom were illiterate. Some members of India’s Constituent Assembly, which drafted the new constitution, had opposed granting universal adult suffrage, asserting that illiteracy and poverty meant that people would fall under the sway of dubious political appeals. In the end, the leadership under Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru challenged that belief and granted every Indian citizen over the age of 21 the right to vote. In 1951-52, independent India held its first free and fair election. As the country’s first chief election commissioner, Sukumar Sen—a civil servant who trained as a mathematician—was responsible for overseeing the voter rolls and ensuring the integrity of the vote. The task he faced was daunting: The Indian electorate at the time was composed of 176 million citizens , 85 percent of whom were illiterate. Some members of India’s Constituent Assembly, which drafted the new constitution, had opposed granting universal adult suffrage, asserting that illiteracy and poverty meant that people would fall under the sway of dubious political appeals. In the end, the leadership under Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru challenged that belief and granted every Indian citizen over the age of 21 the right to vote. More than 60 percent of those eligible voted in India’s first election, and the votes that followed in the next few decades were widely seen as free and fair. But beginning in the 1980s, as the electorate became more politically aware and less predictable, charges of voter fraud from parties across the ideological spectrum became rampant. Literacy was increasing—and with it, voter participation at all levels. Parties could no longer count on predictable political outcomes, leading them to resort to some chicanery. Former Cabinet Secretary T.N. Seshan took office as chief election commissioner in 1990 and lost no time in restoring faith in India’s electoral integrity. To his credit, Seshan implemented existing laws with vigor and without partiality; he enforced rules intensely and helped lead to a decline in election violence. How
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/01/india-modi-protest-election-commission-cockroach-movement/

* Oil Is Again Flowing Out of Hormuz
  Foreign Policy | 01 Oct 17:48 UTC
  But the continued shortfall of refined products means little relief at the pump.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Over the past week, there has been a remarkable turnaround in supplies of crude oil making it out of the still-threatened Strait of Hormuz, with oil volumes returning nearly to prewar levels. What is not leaving the strait—or many parts of Russia, or now China—are refined products such as gasoline and diesel. That means that despite the apparent success of the U.S. naval blockade of Iranian ports and the U.S. Navy’s escorting of a steadily rising stream of tankers out of Hormuz, there will be little relief at the pump for consumers in the United States or Europe for months to come. Over the past week, there has been a remarkable turnaround in supplies of crude oil making it out of the still-threatened Strait of Hormuz, with oil volumes returning nearly to prewar levels. What is not leaving the strait—or many parts of Russia, or now China—are refined products such as gasoline and diesel. That means that despite the apparent success of the U.S. naval blockade of Iranian ports and the U.S. Navy’s escorting of a steadily rising stream of tankers out of Hormuz, there will be little relief at the pump for consumers in the United States or Europe for months to come. The recovery in crude flows out of the Persian Gulf (and, for Saudi Arabia, out of its alternative port on the Red Sea) has been astonishing. From a rough average of about 6 million to 8 million barrels of oil a day in the middle of September, average weekly flows are now nearly double, or about 13.5 million barrels a day, according to shipping firm Kpler. Saudi Arabia, in particular, is outperforming even its 2025 export numbers, with huge amounts of crude exiting the Strait of Hormuz and significant volumes escaping via the Red Sea; Kpler estimates that Saudi crude exports are at least 6.4 million barrels, versus 5.4 million barrels before the war. Estimates of the true recovery of Hormuz flows are notoriously tricky to nail down, because tanker-tracking services all present different numbers, and often over different time frames. But the trend line is clear: Oil is again flowing out of Hormuz. That partial recovery of energy flows after seven months of war has given encouragement to Trump administration officials who were confident that the combination of U.S. escorts and a distance blockade on Iranian vessels, Tehran’s economic lifeblood, would both bring relief to the global economy and compel Iran to seek terms.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/01/oil-strait-hormuz-iran-trump-war-gulf-gas-diesel-prices/

* A Space-Launch Crash Is Coming
  Foreign Policy | 01 Oct 15:32 UTC
  What happens after SpaceX’s most popular vehicle leaves the market?
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The commercial space market is booming after years of surviving on the fringes of a government-dominated industry. The monster SpaceX initial public offering earlier this year demonstrated the deep interest that public markets have in the industry, and a constellation of companies, building everything from satellites to rockets, has matured in its wake. There is a massive incoming problem, however—all of this industry is dependent on the ability to cheaply and reliably move cargo into space, and that is about to get a lot less certain. SpaceX has been synonymous with both launch capacity and infrastructure for years because it launches so much more than anyone else. The U.S. commercial launch industry is basically SpaceX; the government industry is also dependent on SpaceX , and internationally, many others are as well . Partially as a function of this scale, SpaceX is also able to compete on price in ways that other launch providers simply cannot; the Falcon 9 is both cheap and reliable in an industry where neither of those is especially common—that rocket has seen the most launches among active launch vehicles ( roughly 700 ) with a success rate of more than 99 percent.   FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. The last few weeks have launched concerns about an AI extinction event into the mainstream. * Journalist Evan Gershkovich: My 491 Days as Putin’s Political Prisoner
  Longreads | 01 Oct 13:00 UTC
  "Evan Gershkovich, a Wall Street Journal reporter in Russia, thought he was meeting a source. Instead he was arrested for spying."
  FULL TEXT: In this tense, riveting first-person excerpt from his new book This Cursed Beautiful Land: A Russian Story , Evan Gershkovich recounts the harrowing months he spent as a prisoner in Russia, leading up to his eventual release in a complex, multi-country prisoner exchange. Even in the darkness, Gershkovich offers rays of light: care packages from friends, letters by the hundred, and a cautiously blossoming love with a friend in Berlin. We may know the ending, but the details of these days reveal what it takes to get there. A few hours after my arrival, I heard a cart rolling down the hallway. A guard opened a kormushka — a feeder window in the door about the size of a human head. A man in a chef’s uniform filled my bowl with porridge, placed a piece of rancid-smelling boiled fish on my plate and gave me some weak tea. The reality of my new surroundings slammed into me. Thoughts ricocheted inside my head. Earlier in the day I’d had a comforting one: they’ve taken me for a swap, I told myself. That’s all this is. Now, curled up on the bed in my 12ft by 8ft cell, knees pulled to my chest, I thought, what if the FSB really does think I’m a spy and wants to make an example out of me? Maybe they don’t want to swap me at all. Maybe I really will spend 20 years here. “Erik Giggleman was an elite Army soldier who went in search of a righteous struggle and found it in Ukraine. As his commitment deepened, his father confronted an excruciating truth: Either the conflict would end, or Erik would die fighting.” “Why did the Russian state go after an experimental theatre director?” “Forbidden Stories investigated her detention and death, which came on the heels of a reporting trip to Zaporizhzhia aimed at telling the stories of Ukrainian civilians unlawfully held by Russia.” “How a card-playing Siberian AI outsmarted the world’s brightest researchers and raked in millions.” “When Putin invaded, a historian in Kyiv saw that Ukraine’s cultural heritage was in danger. So he set out to save as much of it as he could.” “After Zac Brettler mysteriously plummeted into the Thames, his grieving parents discovered that he’d been posing as an oligarch’s son. Would the police help them solve the puzzle of his death?”
  https://longreads.com/2026/10/01/journalist-putins-political-prisoner/

* I Lost Myself When I Lost Hong Kong
  Longreads | 01 Oct 11:00 UTC
  "If returning to America was like getting on a lifeboat, why did I feel like I was drowning?"
  FULL TEXT: A powerful reflection on the pull of nostalgia, on learning to accept that a past life is gone even as you tentatively reach back for it. Tiffany Huang captures the ache of lost opportunities and regretted paths, of staying a course out of duty and responsibility even as your sense of home erodes. It’s a gorgeous, bittersweet read about what it means to move on long before you’ve fully let go. You unmasked for the first time, revealing smooth cheeks, a sharp chin, and a long nose. You talked about your book, in progress for several years now, about a Hong Kong man drifting aimlessly around the world. I asked you how you felt about the protests and noted the resignation in your tone. You wanted to write a book about what it meant to be a man from Hong Kong; if Hong Kong disappeared, what would happen to the man? You told me about your family, and it was clear we were not meeting to talk about writing anymore. When you spoke, you chose your words carefully. You didn’t ask me about my boyfriend, only how I felt about returning to America. When I hesitated, you did not ask why, and when I spoke, you listened instead of interrupting me. I didn’t want to leave. “Is all the beekeeping, baking and leatherwork just escapist fantasy or the start of a radically human approach to work?” “The country’s legendary main street celebrates its centennial against the backdrop of the nation’s 250th birthday. Could there be a better time for a road trip?” “Inside the tiny community of English master thatchers, a fight is unfolding over a tradition that may not survive.” “Why is a right-wing press reissuing century-old adolescent mystery novels?” “The modern world has made us ill-equipped for the nuisances of past technologies, even as it has fuelled nostalgia for things that might transport us back to calmer times.”

* The Abuses in Turkish Textile Factories Supplying Major US Brands
  New Lines | 01 Oct 10:27 UTC
  In an exclusive investigation, New Lines reports on the Turkish textile factories where women workers face forced overtime and sexual harassment — factories that feed the supply chains for many famous clothing brands across the United States and Europe. The post The Abuses in Turkish Textile Factories Supplying Major US Brands appeared fi...
  FULL TEXT: I met the women on a Sunday, their only day off. The temperature in Malatya, a city in eastern Turkey, was 122 degrees. They had dressed carefully — light makeup, fresh nail polish, their finest headscarves — and as soon as they arrived, they examined each other’s clothing. We sat in a park, and the women sent their children off to play. They were excited to learn that this article would run in an American magazine; the thought that it might reach the people who actually buy the denim clothing they make mattered to them. Before we began, the women asked to use pseudonyms to protect their identities and livelihoods, the fear of retribution always on their minds. So I asked the women to choose names that they felt represented them, not necessarily a different first name from their own, but any noun or description that they felt captured their essence. They quickly caught on. “Green,” one of them said, because it’s her favorite color. “Nature,” said another, because she dreams of returning to her village and living close to nature in retirement. “Tired,” said the third woman. It captured her state most of the time. “What does the factory remind you of?” I asked, posing the question to anyone who wanted to chime in. “A dough machine,” Nature replied. “Like a machine that mixes flour, water and yeast to make dough. Working in the factory is very tiring. You enter as one person and come out as another.” Nature has worked at Baykan Denim for eight years, producing jeans for world-famous brands like Calvin Klein and Tommy Hilfiger. Despite her seniority, she still earns minimum wage, taking home 22,104 Turkish lira (or $533) a month in 2025. Monthly rent in Malatya averaged around 15,000 lira for that year. The Confederation of Turkish Trade Unions, the largest trade union confederation in Turkey, estimated that a single worker needed 39,123 lira, or about $916 a month, to cover basic living costs. An hour of overtime at the legal rate adds only about $4. Nature said that there is little left after basic expenses. Even buying new clothes or going to the cinema can take months of planning. But what hurts most, she said, is the harassment and insults she endures from her superiors at work. “I tell them I don’t want to do overtime. Because of my age, I can’t stand for long periods. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. David Alm | Longreads | October 1, 2026 | 5,726 words (24 minutes) I got off Interstate 81 to give my soul a break. The back roads of southwest Virginia wind, crest, and plunge through lush valleys of oak, poplar, birch, and maple trees. It’s easy to lose track of time and distance there. People drive slower. Cows graze in pastures. And there are no placards festooned with Motel 6 and Starbucks logos, flattening the American landscape into a homogeneous blur. Any signs you might see are low to the ground and handmade, protesting a data center, for instance, or thanking Jesus. I’d gone to this rural pocket of the country for the 90th annual Old Fiddler’s Convention in Galax, an independent city of just under 7,000 near the North Carolina border whose population, for six days in early August, balloons to around four times that. They arrive in RVs and Casitas, camper vans and cars to stake out 20-by-35-foot plots in Felts Park, a 28-acre fairground in the heart of Galax, transforming it into a massive, muddy parking lot sprinkled with portable toilets and a cluster of food trucks, offering loaded potatoes, funnel cake, and blooming onions. The Old Fiddler’s Convention—or just Galax, as the diehards call it—is the oldest and largest fiddlers’ convention in the world, a place where banjo pickers, guitar players, and other maestros of old-time strings gather to see how they stack up. Founded in 1935, Galax has been canceled only twice since then: once during World War II, due to travel restrictions, and again in 2020, due to the COVID-19 pandemic. Many Galaxers have been going every year for decades, some for as long as they’ve been alive. There are scads of rules: If you play clawhammer-style banjo in the bluegrass band competition, you will get disqualified. Same if you play bluegrass-style banjo in the old-time competition. If you break a string while performing, you must stop immediately. Playing time during competition is limited to two minutes and 30 seconds. And, crucially, no electric instruments of any kind are allowed, either on stage or in the tightly packed fairgrounds, where self-described hillbillies, accountants, firefighters, and session musicians pick day and night in blistering, impromptu jams amid the RVs. * Reason is more than a tool
  Aeon | 01 Oct 10:00 UTC
  If intelligence is merely optimisation then machines will outrun us. Kant tells us why human reason is so much more - by Sasha Mudd Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Illustration by Aeon/Daniel Ceng/Anadolu/Getty Images A few months ago, I sat in a packed university auditorium listening to a celebrated computer scientist explain the brilliance of large language models (LLMs). These systems optimise, he said. And they improve at optimisation on the basis of past experience. He paused. That, he said with a smile, is intelligence. The audience nodded along approvingly. Meanwhile, a philosophical alarm bell began sounding in my head. Not because the speaker’s claim was implausible. On the contrary, as a description of much contemporary artificial intelligence, it seemed apt. AI systems are built around objectives, losses, rewards, predictions, preferences, benchmarks and feedback loops. They are trained to perform better relative to some standard of success. In one form or another, they optimise. That is a great part of their technical power. What troubled me was the ease with which a definition appropriate to machines sounded like a definition of intelligence as such. That slide from engineering model to human anthropology is easy to miss. We begin by saying: this is how machines work. Then, almost without noticing, we say: this is what intelligence is. And then, more dangerously still: perhaps this is what we are. An old argument about human beings had entered the room without announcing itself. It sounded, instead, like a definition. The AI revolution has brought one of the Enlightenment’s deepest quarrels back to life – and made one side of it seem like plain common sense. In my view, the wrong side. Hence the alarm bell. I n the 18th century, philosophers were concerned not only with the question of what reason is, but with what it is good for. Is reason essentially a servant, a clever instrument for achieving ends supplied from elsewhere? Or is it sovereign, capable of judging, revising and even legislating ends for itself? Can it ultimately shape the direction of human life, or is it merely along for the ride? Everything – from science to social progress to the viability of the French Revolution – seemed to hang in the balance. David Hume , the cool-eyed Scottish anatomist of human nature, gave the most famous answer on one side: ‘Reason is, and ought only to be the slave of the passions,’ he wrote in A Treatise of Human Nature (1739). It can ‘never pretend to any other office than to serve and obey them.’ Reason does not command. It defers. It does not tell us what to want or what might be worth wanting. Instead, i
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-real-intelligence-is-something-more-than-optimisation

  Lost in a maze or found in a labyrinth? Come, let’s keep walking.

And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. Once, as a young adult in the city, I was walking home after a long day of work. The main street leading to my place wasn’t well lit, with dark stretches between streetlamps. There was a huddle of men under one, some standing around, some lounging against the post. They moved in the orange light, turning as one to face me. Something shifted in the air. An earlier version of me would have been slower to respond, given them the benefit of the doubt. But after a recent knife-point mugging, I had begun to develop a sort of second skin—a keen, physical sense of danger. I slipped back into the shadows and ran in and out of alleyways. I doubled back, cut across fences, and scaled half walls. I made no sound at all; I made it home. I had made a spiraling maze in real time. They could not find me. In his essay “ From the Tree to the Labyrinth ,” Umberto Eco, a lover of labyrinths, explores how we “create and organize knowledge.” Moving away from the restrictive “tree” model, Eco approaches the “labyrinth” model, listing three kinds—a unicursal labyrinth, with only one path to the center and back; a multicursal maze, with many paths, all but one leading to dead ends; and an infinite network of interlinked points, which he calls by the borrowed term “rhizome.” There are many labyrinths and mazes woven into my memory—verbal, textual, graphical, physical, metaphorical. Tamil kolam s , for instance: My mother draws small, perfect threshold patterns at home, and immense, geometrically flawless labyrinths and mazes for events—no outlines, no tools, just her seeing eye and moving hand. When the sky is still dark, she dips folded muslin in wet, ground rice and draws liquid lines on the clean floor. She must draw each bit before the white dries on her hand, which it does almost instantly. Nothing can be erased or redrawn. She must keep moving until the whole kolam is done—the body walking the labyrinth even as it summons it out of nothing, the labyrinth making the world even as it is devoured by little black ants. Eco’s rhizome model, to its credit, says we may never know everything, but we can keep going. Jorge Luis Borges certainly did, over and over like a modern-day Scheherazade. He’d taken time to do the right thing and had visited his primary care provider for a routine health exam and blood tests, his first in many years. A few weeks later, he’d found himself in an unfamiliar urologist’s office, at the edge of an abyss, ears ringing with what may be three of the most dreaded words in medicine: ‘You have cancer.’ This diagnosis portends pain, suffering and death: in the United States alone, cancer kills more than 600,000 people a year, enough lives to empty a city. Yet, receiving a cancer diagnosis doesn’t necessarily mean what it once did. William was in very broad company: more than 2 million Americans a year receive the diagnosis and, for many, the finding entails little to no threat to length or quality of life, except through the ill effects of treatments. However, patients labelled with the ‘C-word’ face substantial clinical, psychological, social and economic consequences. This disconnect – between what the word evokes in my exam room and what it often describes in contemporary practice – drives controversies and variability in screening practices, and in light of our growing understanding of cellular biology, demands that we rethink what we really mean when we apply the label ‘cancer’. William’s cancer was in his prostate, an organ of whose existence he was previously only vaguely aware, but whose removal or radiation risked impairments to urinary, bowel and sexual function. His biopsy had revealed a prostate tumour we designate ‘grade group 1’, which features few molecular hallmarks of cancer and no immediate potential for metastasis or death. Nonetheless, beset with many competing opinions from his rapidly expanding medical team, William found himself rushing headlong toward treatment, and wrestling with a constant anxiety he’d never felt before. By the time he reached my office, he had taken medical leave from work and confided that, perversely, he was smoking for the first time in decades. W hen I started medical school in the 1990s, I found cancer fascinating and terrifying exactly because of the betrayal it represented. These specialized memory chip interfaces allow artificial intelligence processors to access large amounts of data quickly, making them a crucial component of the powerful systems that train and run AI models. Over the past year, Samsung has more than doubled its market share, stealing away SK Hynix’s business while forging new pacts with Nvidia and OpenAI. For U.S. AI companies such as Nvidia and OpenAI, the rivalry matters because control over this critical component could affect how quickly and cheaply they can build the next generation of AI infrastructure. “The question of technology leadership still matters, but now it is also assessed alongside capacity, because the entire memory industry faces constrained production capacity,” said Christopher Miller, associate professor of international history at the Fletcher School of Law and Diplomacy at Tufts University, and author of the bestselling book Chip War: The Fight for the World’s Most Critical Technology. “All the leading DRAM [dynamic random access memory] companies — SK Hynix, Samsung, and Micron — are trying to use deals and long-term agreements with customers to make their HBM products more customized and shift from being a commodity business (as memory traditionally has been) to a more specialized product offering,” Miller told Rest of World . SK Hynix is deepening ties with the U.S. AI industry, including a $4 billion advanced packaging facility in Indiana, where it broke ground in August. Hector raises the boy into the air, says a short prayer, and hands him back. ‘Go home, attend to your own handiwork / at loom and spindle,’ he commands, in Robert Fitzgerald’s translation: ‘As for the war, / that is for men.’ Hector Taking Leave of Andromache (1768) by Angelica Kauffmann. Courtesy Wikipedia Hector is a father in the heroic mode, and his parenting style has long been irresistible to artists and writers. Few were shocked when the painter Paul Gauguin left his wife and five children in Denmark to pursue fame and art in Paris, then Tahiti. Such distance, they say, is good for a child. ‘The father, from his distance, supports, protects, nourishes his child,’ wrote D H Lawrence in 1922: ‘and it is ultimately on the remote but powerful father-love that the infant rests.’ Distance is equally necessary for the father who seeks to make art. ‘There is no more sombre enemy of good art than the pram in the hall,’ wrote Cyril Connolly in Enemies of Promise (1938). This is a deeply self-serving story, of course. It’s nice for the male poet not to have to change diapers. But the argument that unsullied privacy is essential to creativity is tenacious. It surfaces when Virginia Woolf writes that ‘a woman must have money and a room of her own if she is to write fiction.’ Contemporary feminists have continued to respond to this archetype of generativity. In Jenny Offill’s novel Dept. of Speculation (2014), for example, the narrator vows never to marry but to become ‘an art monster instead’. But what if domesticity – and specifically parenthood – is in fact generative for artists? What if the cliché of solitary male creativity got it wrong? We may begin with one nursing poet. The New York Times was the first to report that the club had been found guilty in 114 out of 115 charges of financial misconduct, in a ruling by an independent panel spanning a nine-year period from 2009 to 2018. The club will appeal the ruling, but the potential punishments could range from a monetary fine to stripping the club of its titles and relegation to the lower divisions of English competitions, a drastic step that would lead to the dismantling of one of global soccer’s most successful enterprises. The charges originated with an investigation by German magazine Der Spiegel based on leaked financial documents, which prompted a brief ban for Man City from European competitions and sparked an investigation by the Premier League, which eventually charged the club in 2023. Man City maintains its innocence. Now the saga is coming to a head, and it could have major diplomatic repercussions. Back in August, Bloomberg reported that UAE officials had privately warned that a harsh verdict against the club in the case could damage bilateral relations between the United Kingdom and the UAE, and threaten billions of dollars of existing Emirati investment in the country.   FULL TEXT: For decades after Soviet Premier Nikita Khrushchev said “We will bury you” in 1956, it was widely (though incorrectly) understood as a mad leader threatening America with destruction. Seventy years later, for the first time in history, a U.S. leader unambiguously threatened genocide at the United Nations. It was not a euphemism. When he addressed the U.N. General Assembly on Sept. 22, President Donald Trump was absolutely clear that he was threatening to kill every man, woman and child in Iran — all 92 million of them. “Do I annihilate the Islamic Republic and do it quickly?” he asked, posturing as an absolute monarch with the power to destroy a nation on a whim. “Do I drive them into hell with no chance of survival and no hope of future greatness or generations?” Worse, the only way to do this “quickly” is by using nuclear weapons. There is simply no other way to kill that many people. Horrifyingly, Trump actually has the ability to do this. As president, he has the sole, unfettered ability to launch nuclear weapons whenever he wants, for whatever reason he wants. In the archaic system established by past presidents during the Cold War, Trump can pick up the phone, issue an order, and in five minutes missiles would be flying. Trump’s threat was consistent with his history of declaring that he would destroy all of Iran if it did not surrender. On April 7 of this year, as it became clear that the war he began would not be quick or easy, he declared on social media: “A whole civilization will die tonight, never to be brought back again.” “This would be nuclear genocide,” Tara Drozdenko, director of the Global Security Program at the Union of Concerned Scientists, said about Trump’s latest threats. “Though it may be tempting to write this off as just another bombastic speech, the public must read President Trump’s words, and consider their true meaning and the real human impacts of nuclear war,” she said. * Reasoning together
  Aeon | 25 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Jürgen Habermas, the great defender of deliberative democracy, lived up to its demands: he never feared changing his mind - by Emilie Prattico Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jürgen Habermas at the philosophical faculty of Frankfurt University, Germany, in January 1969. All photos by Max Scheler/Süddeutsche Zeitung/Alamy Jürgen Habermas died on 14 March 2026, in the German town of Starnberg, aged 96. Every fall, from the mid-1990s into the late-2000s, he would come to Northwestern University in Evanston, Illinois to teach and give talks, a visit that a dedicated group of students looked forward to every fall term. The year that has stayed with me is 2004, when he taught what he’d spelled out in Between Facts and Norms (1992), his opus applying his theory to democratic politics. Law, philosophy, history, political science: students spanned many disciplines to come watch the famous German scholar every week, dressed in Wrangler jeans and a tweed jacket, expose his theory of deliberative politics in his unmistakable nasal voice, thick German accent, and impeccable English. Max Weber and the Frankfurt School, which were intimate to him, since he’d been Theodor Adorno’s research assistant in the 1950s, came to life in his speech and then in schemas and figures in chalk as he theorised about them. I understood almost none of it at first: words I’d read were coming to life in the room, spoken by their author, and still not resolving into sense. After several lectures, the shape came into focus: this wasn’t just Between Facts and Norms being rebuilt in public, but decades of work arriving at a single place. Since The Theory of Communicative Action (1981), Habermas had been arguing that reason survives in modern life only as something people do together, that is, by giving each other reasons and staying open to being moved by them, rather than by force, money or tradition. Between Facts and Norms was that idea turned toward law and democracy directly: a state counts as legitimate only when its laws could in principle be justified to everyone bound by them, through exactly that kind of open exchange. I’d watched that argument take root in a decades-long intellectual history. Thinking is a practice, it involves owing each other explanations, questions, answers. It is not a fast process, and doing it in public, out loud, in front of a room, is what makes it worthwhile. I attended all of Habermas’s seminars during my time at Northwestern with great interest and, to be honest, a sense of awe. One day, the topic of the weekly seminar was inclusion, and I had argued, with the timid conviction of a green graduate student, that deliberative publ
  https://aeon.co/essays/habermass-theory-of-truth-meant-he-changed-his-mind

* History and Myth in the Holy Land
  New Lines | 25 Sep 09:53 UTC
  Advocates for the modern state of Israel often invoke the biblical past. A clearer sense of history and myth, including from American leaders, might be one step toward creating a more peaceful Israel-Palestine. The post History and Myth in the Holy Land appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: On Oct. 30, 2024, in the run-up to the presidential election pitting Kamala Harris against Donald Trump, Bill Clinton addressed voters in Michigan, a state with a significant Arab-American population. Democrat strategists were worried that Arab-Americans, along with many students and progressives, would punish their party at the polls for its participation in Israel’s genocide in Gaza. That’s presumably why they brought out Clinton — a former president with star quality — to boost their chances. But Clinton was so lost in his own story that he surely made things worse. His words also revealed the extent to which even elite Americans understand the Middle East through a mythical rather than historical framework. “I understand why young Palestinians and Arab Americans here in Michigan think too many people have died. I get that,” Clinton started, before exonerating the genocidaires of blame. Hamas, he explained, “force you to kill civilians if you want to defend yourself.” Then his discourse degenerated further. “I got news for them,” he said, meaning Hamas. “They [the Israelis] were there first, before their faith existed.” Clinton seems to have meant that the Israelis were “there first” because Judaism is an older religion than Islam, but Judaism is not the Israelis and Islam is not the Palestinians. This strange category error emerges from the same myth complex that renders many Americans unable to distinguish between the Land of Israel imagined in Sunday school and the State of Israel established by conquest and mass ethnic cleansing a few years after World War II. Likewise, they fail to distinguish the ancient Israelites from the contemporary Jewish peoples. The former president would have been better informed had he read some books on the topic other than the Old Testament — books like Nur Masalha’s meticulous and lavishly referenced “The Ancient Cities and Global Trade of Palestine: A 7,000-Year History.” This is the latest in a string of books Masalha has written examining the country’s ancient past, adding a wealth of detail to show the truly multiethnic past of the countries we now call Israel and Palestine. It’s interesting that Clinton, in American terms a secular liberal, arrives at similar conclusions to America’s Christian Zionists, who read Genesis 12:3 — “And I will bless them that bless thee, and curse him that curseth thee” — as an exhortation to support the Israeli state.
